290:    {\scriptsize 1{,}100 params}
361:\caption{\textbf{RR-MoA architecture.} \textit{Path~A} (blue): frozen TSFM produces $\mathbf{H}$ for all experts. \textit{Path~B} (orange): Conv1d gate (1{,}100 params) reads $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$ and selects Top-2 experts; inactive experts (gray) receive no gradient. Standard hidden-state MoE collapses (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}); RR-MoA wins \textbf{54/54} (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}).}
381:where $G_\psi: \mathbb{R}^{T} \to \mathbb{R}^K$ is a small Conv1d\,+\,pooling\,+\,linear gate ($\sim$1.1K params) operating directly on the raw time series, $\mathcal{T} \subset \{1,\ldots,K\}$ is the set of Top-$k$ expert indices, and each $\mathrm{Expert}_j$ is a canonical adapter head. By construction $\sum_j \widetilde{w}_j = 1$ with support only on $\mathcal{T}$, so only $k$ of $K$ experts contribute per sample (the remaining $K{-}k$ are masked out and do not execute). With $K{=}5$, $k{=}2$ we obtain $40\%$ of the dense ($k{=}K$) expert FLOPs while retaining $63\%$ of the dense MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter (\S\ref{sec:topk}). The raw input preserves the temporal statistics (trend, amplitude, volatility) that RevIN strips away, enabling per-sample routing that reflects the physical characteristics of each time series window.
```

By construction $\sum_j \widetilde{w}_j = 1$ with support only on $\mathcal{T}$, so only $k$ of $K$ experts contribute per sample (the remaining $K{-}k$ are masked out and do not execute). With $K{=}5$, $k{=}2$ we obtain $40\%$ of the dense ($k{=}K$) expert FLOPs while retaining $63\%$ of the dense MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter (\S\ref{sec:topk}). The raw input preserves the temporal statistics (trend, amplitude, volatility) that RevIN strips away, enabling per-sample routing that reflects the physical characteristics of each time series window.
---also checking the benchmark line:
The RR-MoA routing overhead ($+3.1$\,ms) is dominated by the sparse expert selection loop; the router itself (Conv1d + linear, ${\sim}1.1$K params) adds $<0.1$\,ms. SR-MoA dense and Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} both run \emph{faster} than RR-MoA Top-2 on GPU because their dense per-expert execution parallelizes over the $K{=}5$ expert dimension without per-sample sparse-routing kernel launches. Notably, Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} has \emph{fewer} adapter parameters than RR-MoA ($354$K vs $426$K) thanks to the shared NLinear raw branch.
```

490	
491	In contrast, \textbf{RR-MoA} maintains healthy routing entropy ($1.0$--$1.57$) and wins \textbf{54/54} configurations, with MSE improvements of $26\%$ to $79\%$ over the best fixed adapter. Notably, on Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, where the stripped statistics carry little information), RR-MoA does not improve over fixed adapters, exactly as Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}).
492	
493	\textbf{Rescue-baseline sweep.} To rule out that the collapse is merely under-tuned, we sweep five standard MoE rescue families (Switch load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, entropy regularization, ReMoE ReLU routing~\citep{wang2025remoe}, and expert-choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}) across twelve configurations on 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 5 seeds $= 720$ runs (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}, Appendix~\ref{app:rescue_details}). The best rescue (z-loss $c_z{=}0.01$) reduces MSE by only $10.9\%$, still $2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA. Excessive balancing ($\alpha{=}10.0$) actively hurts MSE ($+67.7\%$). This confirms Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii): when routing information is structurally absent from the hidden states, none of the standard rescue mechanisms we tested can compensate.
494	
495	\textbf{The principle is architecture-agnostic.} The diagnosis predicts the same fix should work for any router that reads raw input. We validate this with three router-level controls. \textbf{SR-MoA}~\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree} (Self-Routed MoA, Figure~\ref{fig:srmoa_arch}) drops the external router entirely: each expert has its own sigmoid gate $\sigma_k(\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw})$. SR-MoA \emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets ($252$ runs, six backbones) and replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen best on 4/6; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Gating the same SR-MoA architecture on hidden states degrades by $+87$--$768\%$ on all 6 datasets ($p{=}0.016$); on Timer-XL (no RevIN) the same raw-vs-hidden comparison yields only $+4$--$9\%$ ($15$--$100{\times}$ smaller), confirming the effect is normalization-induced. \textbf{AdaMix-Raw} (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}) swaps AdaMix's hidden-state router for a raw-input router and recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\%$ on all 12 cells. \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}) extends the principle to the expert level via a shared NLinear raw branch + gated backbone residual, closing the DLinear gap. Table~\ref{tab:causal_controls} summarizes all eight controls.
496	
497	\begin{table}[h]
498	\centering
499	\caption{\textbf{Eight causal controls.} Each rules out a competing explanation; the only mechanism consistent with all 8 is that \emph{instance normalization strips the per-window $(\mu,\sigma)$ signal the router needs}.}
```

1623	\textbf{Extreme collapse on Exchange and Solar.} The collapse pattern extends to the two newly evaluated datasets (Table~\ref{tab:exchange_solar}). On Solar (137 channels), last-4 unfreezing produces the most extreme collapse in the paper: routing entropy drops to exactly $0.000$ and MSE degrades by $+215$--$243\%$ relative to the best fixed baseline, far worse than the $+0$--$17\%$ degradation observed on the original 6 datasets. On Exchange (8 channels), last-4 AdaMix collapses to $0.000$ entropy but MSE actually improves ($-21\%$), acting as a reasonable single-expert adapter; however, RR-MoA still outperforms it by $2.7\times$ ($1.44$ vs $3.85$).

-\textbf{Rescue-baseline sweep.} To rule out that the collapse is merely under-tuned, we sweep five standard MoE rescue families (Switch load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, entropy regularization, ReMoE ReLU routing~\citep{wang2025remoe}, and expert-choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}) across twelve configurations on 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 5 seeds $= 720$ runs (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}, Appendix~\ref{app:rescue_details}). This confirms Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii): when routing information is structurally absent from the hidden states, none of the standard rescue mechanisms we tested can compensate.
+\textbf{Rescue-baseline sweep.} To rule out that the collapse is merely under-tuned, we sweep five standard MoE rescue families (Switch load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, entropy regularization, ReMoE ReLU routing~\citep{wang2025remoe}, and expert-choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}) across eleven new rescue configurations plus the load-balance baseline already in Table~\ref{tab:adamix} (twelve configurations total) on 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 5 seeds $= 720$ run-cells (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}, Appendix~\ref{app:rescue_details}). The best rescue (z-loss $c_z{=}0.01$) reduces MSE by only $10.9\%$, still $2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA. Excessive balancing ($\alpha{=}10.0$) actively hurts MSE ($+67.7\%$). This confirms Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii): when routing information is structurally absent from the hidden states, none of the standard rescue mechanisms we tested can compensate.
 
 \textbf{The principle is architecture-agnostic.} The diagnosis predicts the same fix should work for any router that reads raw input. We validate this with three router-level controls. \textbf{SR-MoA}~\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree} (Self-Routed MoA, Figure~\ref{fig:srmoa_arch}) drops the external router entirely: each expert has its own sigmoid gate $\sigma_k(\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw})$. SR-MoA \emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets ($252$ runs, six backbones) and replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen best on 4/6; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Gating the same SR-MoA architecture on hidden states degrades by $+87$--$768\%$ on all 6 datasets ($p{=}0.016$); on Timer-XL (no RevIN) the same raw-vs-hidden comparison yields only $+4$--$9\%$ ($15$--$100{\times}$ smaller), confirming the effect is normalization-induced. \textbf{AdaMix-Raw} (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}) swaps AdaMix's hidden-state router for a raw-input router and recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\%$ on all 12 cells. \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}) extends the principle to the expert level via a shared NLinear raw branch + gated backbone residual, closing the DLinear gap. @@ -1126,6 +1126,10 @@ Consider a linear-readout router on the interpolated input $\mathbf{x}_\alpha =
 \end{enumerate}
 \end{proposition}
 
+\begin{proof}[Heuristic derivation]
+Under $M\perp\!\!\!\perp\Sigma\perp\!\!\!\perp S$ jointly Gaussian, $\mathbf{x}_\alpha = (1{-}\alpha)(M + \Sigma S) + \alpha S$. The location-scale component $(1{-}\alpha)(M+\Sigma S)$ contributes routing-discriminative variance $V_\mathrm{sig}(\alpha) = (1{-}\alpha)\,[\mathrm{Var}(M){+}\mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)]$ (the $(1{-}\alpha)$ rather than $(1{-}\alpha)^2$ scaling reflects each component's marginal contribution to a linear-readout router's input variance, see Remark below); the shape component contributes routing-irrelevant variance $V_\mathrm{noise}(\alpha) = \alpha\,\overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)} + V_0$. \textbf{Part (a):} Setting $V_\mathrm{sig}(\alpha^*) = V_\mathrm{noise}(\alpha^*)$ in the limit $V_0\to 0$ gives $(1{-}\alpha^*)[\mathrm{Var}(M){+}\mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)] = \alpha^* \overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$. Dividing both sides by $\overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ and substituting $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M){+}\mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)]/\overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ yields $(1{-}\alpha^*)R = \alpha^*$, so $\alpha^* = R/(R+1)$. Continuity of MSE in $\alpha$ for $\alpha\in[0,1)$ follows from continuity of the softmax composed with a linear function of $\alpha$. \textbf{Part (b):} At $\alpha=1$, $\mathbf{x}_1 = S$ and the routing logits are $\mathbf{w}^\top G_\psi(S)$, a deterministic function of $S$ alone; under $(M,\Sigma)\perp\!\!\!\perp S$, by the data processing inequality $I(S; E_\text{optimal}) \leq I(S; (M,\Sigma)) = 0$, so the router carries zero information about the latent location-scale that drives the optimal routing decision. The softmax of a $S$-only function converges to a single distribution at the population level, decoupling entropy from MSE. The discontinuity at $\alpha=1$ is the limit $V_\mathrm{sig}(1) = 0$ rather than a finite-$\alpha^*$ phase transition.
+\end{proof}
+
 \emph{Remark on the variance comparison.} Comparing the \emph{linear} variance contributions $V_\mathrm{sig}$ and $V_\mathrm{noise}$ (rather than the squared coefficients in $\|\mathbf{x}_\alpha\|^2$) corresponds to comparing each component's marginal contribution to the router's discriminative variance under the orthogonality assumption $M\perp\!\!\!\perp\Sigma\perp\!\!\!\perp S$. Comparing squared coefficients instead would yield $\alpha^* = \sqrt{R}/(\sqrt{R}+1)$; for the median $R{=}0.67$, the two formulas predict $\alpha^* \approx 0.40$ vs $0.45$, and the dose-response grid (5 points) is too coarse to discriminate. We label Proposition~\ref{prop:phase_transition} as a \emph{heuristic} prediction and report the formula consistent with the linear comparison.
 
 \emph{Validation.} For the median $R(\mathcal{D}) = 0.67$, Proposition~\ref{prop:phase_transition}(a) predicts a soft onset $\alpha^* \approx 0.40$. The dose-response experiment (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; 150 runs) is consistent: MSE degrades smoothly by $+5.4\%$ for $\alpha \leq 0.75$ (covering the predicted onset region), and jumps to $+283\%$ at $\alpha = 1.0$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:phase_transition}(b), softmax saturation). The two thresholds are distinct: $\alpha^*$ marks where SNR-driven degradation \emph{begins}; the actual phase transition is at $\alpha = 1$, driven by $V_\mathrm{sig}$ vanishing rather than by SNR crossing.

Note: conv1d (purple) is in the legend but rarely the top-1 expert on ETTh1, so it is not visually present.}

A single RR-MoA run takes $\sim$1--3 minutes per dataset on MOMENT-small, $\sim$1.5 minutes on MOMENT-large, and similar on Moirai/Moirai-MoE/Chronos/Timer-XL. The full experimental suite ($\sim$5{,}000 result files across the main tables, baselines, rescue sweep, cross-backbone, multi-horizon, imputation, and ablations) accumulated approximately 100--200 GPU-hours of compute end-to-end. Inference latency benchmarks (Table~\ref{tab:benchmark}) report wall-clock measurements for each architectural variant.
/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/main.tex:1020:\caption{\textbf{Inference latency and memory across all three architectural variants} (MOMENT-small, A10G GPU, batch size $128$, $200$ repeats with $20$-step CUDA-event warmup; synthetic random inputs at the standard LTSF batch shape, since latency for non-autoregressive deterministic forward passes is data-oblivious). All three architectural fixes (RR-MoA, SR-MoA, Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) add only single-digit milliseconds of adapter overhead on top of the backbone forward pass; the frozen-backbone fast-inference property is preserved by each.}
/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/main.tex:1219:We sweep LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} across three axes to ensure the comparison in Table~\ref{tab:baselines} is not cherry-picked: rank $r{\in}\{8,16,32\}$, target projections ${\in}\{q{+}v,\; q{+}k{+}v{+}o\}$, and forecast head ${\in}\{$linear, 2-layer MLP$\}$, for 12 configurations $\times$ 3 seeds $=$ 36 runs per dataset (108 runs total across the 3 datasets). All runs use a strictly frozen backbone (matching Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}'s primary setting). The trio \{ETTh1, ETTm1, Weather\} covers an hourly ETT dataset, a 15-min ETT dataset, and a multivariate dataset with $21$ channels; we restrict the LoRA sweep to this trio to keep total compute bounded ($\sim 3.6$ GPU-hours), since the broader 6-dataset grid is already covered for every other baseline in Table~\ref{tab:baselines}.
/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/main.tex:1765:\textbf{Compute overhead.} The entire RR-MoA stack (${\sim}400$K adapter FLOPs dense, ${\sim}16$K router FLOPs) is ${\sim}0.003\%$ of a MOMENT-small forward pass (${\sim}15$ GFLOPs) and ${\sim}0.001\%$ for Top-2. On MOMENT-large the ratio drops below $0.0002\%$.

does it sound more impressive now

> AGENT

Yes — meaningfully. Three things to flag.

**The 235 GPU-hours number is genuinely more impressive than 100–200.** It's higher (more compute spent), and it's *measured* rather than estimated. A reviewer who checks reproducibility will find the released JSONs sum to that figure, which signals rigor. "Approximately 100–200" reads like a hand-wave; "5,463 files, ~235 GPU-hours, measured by summing per-run wall-clock from result metadata" reads like someone who actually instrumented their experiments.

**The per-backbone breakdown is more useful than "similar".** Telling the reader Chronos is ~15s and Moirai-MoE is ~215s (a ~14× spread) lets them budget their own reproduction; "similar" was both inaccurate and unhelpful. The fact that Moirai-MoE is the slowest (because it's a sparse-MoE backbone) is also subtly useful context for the cross-backbone discussion.

**One small caveat about whether to push it further.** 235 GPU-hours on a single A10G is solid for a NeurIPS reproducibility statement, but it is not headline-grabbing for a 2026 deadline (papers in this space routinely cite thousands of H100-hours). I would *not* try to inflate it — the strength of this claim is its precision and verifiability, not its magnitude. If anything, lean harder into the "measured, not estimated" framing in the rebuttal if a reviewer questions compute sufficiency.

No edit needed unless you want me to surface the "5,463 files, summed from result metadata" framing more prominently elsewhere (e.g., the abstract or §4 setup paragraph).

## Changes to item 8 framing

| Element | Before | Now |
|---|---|---|
| Source attribution | "approximately 100–200 GPU-hours" (estimated) | "Per-run training wall-clock... is recorded by every experiment script into the per-run result JSON via an `elapsed` field; the figures below are *measured* medians from the released metadata, not estimates." |
| Total compute | "approximately 100–200 GPU-hours" | "**234.8 GPU-hours** of training compute end-to-end" with the formula stated: "summing the `elapsed` field across the 5,045 files that carry it" |
| Per-backbone runtimes | qualitative, with sample size hidden | each line now carries `n=` (1,094 / 15 / 42 / 78 / 90 / 66 runs aggregated) so the median has provenance |
| Reproducibility | implicit | explicit closing sentence: "Both numbers can be re-derived from the released artifacts: the per-run JSONs in `results/` carry the `elapsed` field, and the inference benchmark is reproduced by `scripts/benchmark_inference.py`" |
| Caveats | none | "this excludes orchestrator overhead, model loading, and the few hundred files from legacy runs that pre-date the `elapsed` convention" — pre-empts a reviewer asking why 5,463 ≠ 5,045 |

The hard "234.8" number plus the recipe for re-deriving it is the strongest move: a skeptical reviewer can run a one-liner over `results/*/*.json` and confirm. That's the difference between "trust me" and "verify me."

381:where $G_\psi: \mathbb{R}^{T} \to \mathbb{R}^K$ is a small Conv1d\,+\,pooling\,+\,linear gate ($853$ params) operating directly on the raw time series, $\mathcal{T} \subset \{1,\ldots,K\}$ is the set of Top-$k$ expert indices, and each $\mathrm{Expert}_j$ is a canonical adapter head. By construction $\sum_j \widetilde{w}_j = 1$ with support only on $\mathcal{T}$, so only $k$ of $K$ experts contribute per sample (the remaining $K{-}k$ are masked out and do not execute). With $K{=}5$, $k{=}2$ we obtain $40\%$ of the dense ($k{=}K$) expert FLOPs while retaining $63\%$ of the dense MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter (\S\ref{sec:topk}). The raw input preserves the temporal statistics (trend, amplitude, volatility) that RevIN strips away, enabling per-sample routing that reflects the physical characteristics of each time series window.
551:\textbf{Closing the DLinear gap.} DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in absolute MSE (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, grey rows): the gap reflects information destruction by the normalization-encoding pipeline (k-NN/Ridge diagnostics, Appendix~\ref{app:diagnostic}), not adapter capacity. Restoring raw-signal access \emph{at the expert level} closes it: \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (shared NLinear raw branch $+$ per-expert gated backbone residual; Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}) matches or beats DLinear on $6/6$ datasets at $H{=}96$ (up to $-7.5\%$) and $107/123$ cells across six backbones; a self-routed SR-RIA\textsuperscript{+} (Appendix~\ref{app:sr_ria}) hits the same $6/6$ with $3$ outright wins, and a TSFM-free Pure Raw-MLP MoE control (Appendix~\ref{app:raw_mlp_moe}) localizes the TSFM's contribution.
983:\item \textbf{DLinear}~\citep{zeng2023dlinear}: from-scratch supervised; we use the simplified single-Linear variant ($\text{Linear}(L{=}512 \to H{=}96)$, $49$K params), without the trend-seasonal decomposition, as the calibration anchor. Calibration values $0.416 / 0.322 / 0.208$ on ETTh1/ETTm1/Weather (gap to RR-MoA visible in Tables~\ref{tab:rrmoa},~\ref{tab:baselines}).
1038:The RR-MoA routing overhead ($+3.1$\,ms) is dominated by the sparse expert selection loop; the router itself (Conv1d + linear, $853$ params) adds $<0.1$\,ms. SR-MoA dense and Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} both run \emph{faster} than RR-MoA Top-2 on GPU because their dense per-expert execution parallelizes over the $K{=}5$ expert dimension without per-sample sparse-routing kernel launches. Notably, Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} has \emph{fewer} adapter parameters than RR-MoA ($354$K vs $426$K) thanks to the shared NLinear raw branch.
1846:To test whether gradient descent independently discovers Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}'s prediction, we parameterize the router input as $\mathbf{r} = \sigma(\ell)\,\text{RevIN}(\mathbf{X}) + (1 - \sigma(\ell))\,\mathbf{X}_{\text{raw}}$, where $\ell$ is a scalar \texttt{nn.Parameter} initialized to $0$ (so $\alpha = \sigma(0) = 0.5$). All other settings match the standard RR-MoA config (MOMENT-small, frozen, Top-2, $K{=}5$, $H{=}96$, 15 epochs).
1927:\caption{\textbf{Few-shot learning curve} (H=96, mean$\pm$std over seeds $\{42,43,44\}$). DLinear (49K params, trained from scratch) dominates frozen RR-MoA-full (426K params) at every tested $N \in \{10,\ldots,5000\}$, confirming the gap reflects encoder information loss rather than data efficiency.}
1989:\caption{\textbf{Negative control: Timer-XL}~\citep{liu2025timerxl} (decoder-only, 84M params, LayerNorm only, no RevIN; H=96, 5 seeds). \textbf{Key causal result}: on MOMENT (RevIN), unfreezing drives entropy DOWN toward $0.000$ ($71\%$ collapsed). On Timer-XL, unfreezing drives entropy \textbf{UP} toward the maximum $1.609$ ($0/90$ collapsed). The trajectories are \emph{opposite}, isolating RevIN as the causal mechanism. AdaMix wins $5/6$ when frozen, confirming RR-MoA is unnecessary without instance normalization.}
2128:\textbf{Training-length ablation.} We additionally ran dual-stream at 50 epochs (vs.\ 15 in Table~\ref{tab:gap_closing}). Extended training \emph{overfits} on 5/6 datasets (ETTh1: $0.453{\to}0.556$; Weather: $0.198{\to}0.237$), confirming that dual-stream converges by 15 epochs and that the original training schedule is already near-optimal; additional adapter-only optimization past convergence degrades generalization due to the small effective parameter count ($\sim$718K trainable, frozen backbone).
2354:The two hypotheses make opposite interventions: H1 predicts that \emph{more data} (raising \texttt{max\_samples} to $100{,}000$) closes the gap; H2 predicts that \emph{fewer parameters} (sharing the raw branch across experts to reach DLinear's $49$K budget) closes it. We ran both as single-lever experiments in parallel (Batches~1a and~1b, $5$ seeds each).
2358:\emph{Shared raw branch succeeds.} At $K{=}5$ sharing a single $\text{Linear}(512, 96)$ module, raw parameters drop from $245$K to $49$K, exactly matching DLinear's budget. ETTh1 closes from $+5.1\%$ (sig loss) to $+1.6\%$ (marginal, $p{=}0.086$); ETTh2 closes from $+10.6\%$ (sig loss) to $+7.0\%$ (marginal, $p{=}0.078$). Per-sample gates and distinct backbone branches remain per-expert, so expert specialization is preserved in \emph{how} the backbone residual is invoked; only the DLinear-equivalent base predictor is shared. The co-occurrence of H1's failure and H2's success identifies parameter bloat, not data scarcity, as the root cause.
2555:\textbf{Architecture.} The Pure Raw-MLP MoE consists of $K{=}5$ size-diverse two-layer MLPs operating directly on the $512$-dimensional raw input window: hidden widths $\{64, 96, 128, 192, 256\}$ chosen so that the total parameter count ($449{,}557$) matches RR-MoA's $426$K within $6\%$. Each expert is $\text{Linear}(512{\to}h_k) \to \text{GELU} \to \text{Linear}(h_k{\to}96)$. The router is the same Conv1d $+$ AdaptiveAvgPool $+$ Linear architecture as RR-MoA, also operating on the raw input (not on hidden states). Top-2 sparse routing is used. Training protocol is identical to RR-MoA: Adam $1{\times}10^{-3}$, 15 epochs, batch 128. \textbf{No TSFM forward pass executes anywhere in this architecture}: the ${\sim}40$M-parameter MOMENT-small backbone is removed entirely.

64:Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, and Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} now provide powerful pretrained representations for time series, with several recent variants pushing toward billion-parameter scales~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm, liu2026timers1}. Using one of these backbones on a downstream task still requires attaching a lightweight adapter head that maps hidden states to predictions, and the design of that adapter, more than the backbone choice, determines deployment utility.
389:\textbf{Datasets and tasks.} Six LTSF benchmarks (ETTh1/h2/m1/m2~\citep{zhou2021informer}, Weather, Electricity~\citep{godahewa2021monash}) with chronological splits and channel-wise normalization~\citep{nie2023patchtst}. We evaluate forecasting ($H{=}96$) and imputation ($20\%$ masked).
391:\textbf{Backbones.} Five backbones spanning two normalization families: instance-normalized MOMENT-small/large~\citep{goswami2024moment} (internal RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin}); LayerNorm-only Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} and Moirai-MoE; and Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos} and Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl} as no-instance-normalization negative controls (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). MOMENT-small is primary. Primary results use a strictly frozen backbone; freeze-level ablations follow.
655:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Cross-backbone, task, horizon.}\label{sec:cross_backbone}\label{sec:topk} RR-MoA wins 21/21 MOMENT-small/large ($26$--$79\%$), 18/18 Moirai ($3$--$31\%$), 6/6 Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} ($37$--$90\%$; Tables~\ref{tab:cross_backbone},~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}) — adapter- and backbone-internal routing are complementary. Predicted negative controls (Chronos, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, no instance norm) show no collapse and no gain (4/18 within chance; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_backbone}). Top-2 routing retains most of the dense gain (Appendix~\ref{app:topk}); imputation $7/8$ (Appendix~\ref{app:imputation}) and horizons $H{\in}\{96,192,336,720\}$ $24/24$ (Table~\ref{tab:horizon}) confirm robustness; Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} extends to 6 datasets $\times$ 5 backbones $\times$ 4 horizons (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}).}
710:\bibitem[Liu et~al.(2025b)]{liu2025timerxl}
860:\bibitem[Zhou et~al.(2021)]{zhou2021informer}
865:\bibitem[Godahewa et~al.(2021)]{godahewa2021monash}
973:\paragraph{Training hyperparameters.} Adam optimizer, learning rate $10^{-3}$, MSE loss, batch size $128$, $15$ epochs. Final reported numbers are 15-epoch test MSE. Core multi-seed grids use seeds $\{42, 43, 44, 45, 46\}$ ($n{=}5$); ablations use seeds $\{42, 43, 44\}$ ($n{=}3$). Channel-wise StandardScaler normalization~\citep{zhou2021informer, nie2023patchtst} is applied to all inputs.
1989:\caption{\textbf{Negative control: Timer-XL}~\citep{liu2025timerxl} (decoder-only, 84M params, LayerNorm only, no RevIN; H=96, 5 seeds). \textbf{Key causal result}: on MOMENT (RevIN), unfreezing drives entropy DOWN toward $0.000$ ($71\%$ collapsed). On Timer-XL, unfreezing drives entropy \textbf{UP} toward the maximum $1.609$ ($0/90$ collapsed). The trajectories are \emph{opposite}, isolating RevIN as the causal mechanism. AdaMix wins $5/6$ when frozen, confirming RR-MoA is unnecessary without instance normalization.}
1990:\label{tab:timer_xl}

571:In the corner case where routing signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics ($E$ deterministic in $(M,\Sigma)$, $(M,\Sigma)\perp\!\!\!\perp S$; Part~(iii), Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}), $I(X;E){-}I(S;E)=H(E)$: RevIN destroys the entire routing distribution. We use the \emph{signal ratio} $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M)+\mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)]/\overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ as a tractable monotone proxy. The bound is empirically tight (CCA $\varepsilon\in[0.09,1.26]$ nats, KSG-validated~\citep{kraskov2004ksg}; gap $0.075$ nats, Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}); learnable $\alpha$ converges to $\alpha{<}0.5$ on $30/30$ runs (Appendix~\ref{app:learnable_alpha}). $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts raw-routing benefit at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}), whereas $H(E\mid S)$ alone does not ($\rho{=}{-}0.12$). Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, $\Delta{=}{+}2.9\%$) is correctly flagged a boundary case; Solar ($R{=}0.06$) is the one outlier (SSR $+43\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).
1526:\textbf{Sufficient statistic router (SSR).} A router using only $[\mu(X_\text{raw}), \sigma(X_\text{raw})]$, functionally equivalent to a DishTS-style~\citep{fan2023dishts} routing mechanism that conditions on the exact statistics distribution-shift methods re-inject, degrades MSE by $+48\%$ on average ($+11\%$ to $+94\%$) vs.\ the Conv1d router across all 8 forecasting datasets. The degradation is largest on ETTm2 ($+94\%$) and Exchange ($+82\%$), and smallest on Weather ($+11\%$). While Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} correctly identifies the \emph{direction} of the information loss, effective routing requires temporal shape patterns beyond location-scale statistics.
```

75:\item We formalize the mechanism via a mutual-information decomposition (Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) and derive a tractable signal-ratio predictor $R(\mathcal{D})$ that anticipates which datasets benefit from raw routing at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$), including boundary cases where it correctly does not (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$). The predictor generalizes across three normalization families, five backbones, and a vision modality.
491:In contrast, \textbf{RR-MoA} maintains healthy routing entropy ($1.0$--$1.57$) and wins \textbf{54/54} configurations, with MSE improvements of $26\%$ to $79\%$ over the best fixed adapter. Notably, on Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, where the stripped statistics carry little information), RR-MoA does not improve over fixed adapters, exactly as Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}).
519:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Two distinct gains.} \emph{(Q1)} the best fixed adapter on the same RevIN-normalized hidden states still loses to RR-MoA by $26$--$79\%$ (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}) — residual shape heterogeneity survives normalization and rewards diverse experts; \emph{(Q2)} the signal-ratio $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ measures \emph{dispatch}-signal localization in the stripped statistics — why dense routing also benefits from raw access, and why Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) correctly does not improve. The Pure Raw-MLP MoE ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:raw_mlp_moe}) shows the dataset-conditional choice between ``MoE with raw router'' and ``ensemble with raw input''.}
571:In the corner case where routing signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics ($E$ deterministic in $(M,\Sigma)$, $(M,\Sigma)\perp\!\!\!\perp S$; Part~(iii), Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}), $I(X;E){-}I(S;E)=H(E)$: RevIN destroys the entire routing distribution. We use the \emph{signal ratio} $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M)+\mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)]/\overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ as a tractable monotone proxy. The bound is empirically tight (CCA $\varepsilon\in[0.09,1.26]$ nats, KSG-validated~\citep{kraskov2004ksg}; gap $0.075$ nats, Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}); learnable $\alpha$ converges to $\alpha{<}0.5$ on $30/30$ runs (Appendix~\ref{app:learnable_alpha}). $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts raw-routing benefit at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}), whereas $H(E\mid S)$ alone does not ($\rho{=}{-}0.12$). Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, $\Delta{=}{+}2.9\%$) is correctly flagged a boundary case; Solar ($R{=}0.06$) is the one outlier (SSR $+43\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).
627:% Boundary cases: Traffic (low R, no benefit predicted) and Solar (outlier).
649:\caption{\textbf{Left: Frozen Paradox}, frozen RR-MoA beats full FT by $12$--$79\%$. \textbf{Right: Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} validated} ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$). Traffic and Solar are boundary cases.}
661:MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization needed for distribution shift also strips the per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it, motivates Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}, and $R(\mathcal{D})$ identifies where it applies. The broader pattern: when a foundation model's input pipeline destroys signal a downstream router needs, the fix is restoring the input, not retuning the optimizer (replicated in a vision control, Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}). \textbf{Limits:} \emph{(i)}~instance-level normalization only (attention-based/learned normalizers uncharacterized); \emph{(ii)}~$R(\mathcal{D})$ is a single monotone proxy, not sharp on shape-statistics-coupled data (Solar, $R{=}0.06$); \emph{(iii)}~forecasting and imputation only.\label{page:end_main}
1099:\emph{Gaussian reference.} Since $(M,\Sigma)$ is 2-dimensional, there are at most 2 canonical correlations $\rho_1, \rho_2$ between $(M,\Sigma)$ and the shape $S$. For jointly Gaussian variables, $I(M,\Sigma; S) = -\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^2 \log(1 - \rho_i^2)$. This yields $\varepsilon_\text{Gauss}$ ranging from $0.09$ nats (Weather, $\rho_\text{max}{=}0.35$) to $1.26$ nats (Exchange, $\rho_\text{max}{=}0.89$). The leakage is \emph{not} uniformly small: datasets with strong trend-seasonality coupling (Exchange, ETTh2) have substantial shape-level encoding of location-scale statistics.
1103:\emph{Tightness condition.} The bound~(ii) is informative when $I(M,\Sigma; E) \gg \varepsilon$. Since $I(M,\Sigma; E)$ increases with the location-scale variance, the signal ratio $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M) + \mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)] / \overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ is a monotone proxy for the tightness of the bound. This explains the $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ correlation: high-$R$ datasets (Exchange, $R{=}2.17$) have $I(M,\Sigma; E) \gg \varepsilon$, producing large gains ($-68.5\%$); low-$R$ datasets (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$) have $I(M,\Sigma; E) \approx \varepsilon$, producing no gain ($+2.9\%$).
1526:\textbf{Sufficient statistic router (SSR).} A router using only $[\mu(X_\text{raw}), \sigma(X_\text{raw})]$, functionally equivalent to a DishTS-style~\citep{fan2023dishts} routing mechanism that conditions on the exact statistics distribution-shift methods re-inject, degrades MSE by $+48\%$ on average ($+11\%$ to $+94\%$) vs.\ the Conv1d router across all 8 forecasting datasets. The degradation is largest on ETTm2 ($+94\%$) and Exchange ($+82\%$), and smallest on Weather ($+11\%$). While Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} correctly identifies the \emph{direction} of the information loss, effective routing requires temporal shape patterns beyond location-scale statistics.
1623:\textbf{Extreme collapse on Exchange and Solar.} The collapse pattern extends to the two newly evaluated datasets (Table~\ref{tab:exchange_solar}). On Solar (137 channels), last-4 unfreezing produces the most extreme collapse in the paper: routing entropy drops to exactly $0.000$ and MSE degrades by $+215$--$243\%$ relative to the best fixed baseline, far worse than the $+0$--$17\%$ degradation observed on the original 6 datasets. On Exchange (8 channels), last-4 AdaMix collapses to $0.000$ entropy but MSE actually improves ($-21\%$), acting as a reasonable single-expert adapter; however, RR-MoA still outperforms it by $2.7\times$ ($1.44$ vs $3.85$).
1737:\textbf{ResNet-18: normalization prevents collapse.} Without normalization (C), the unfrozen backbone co-adapts with the dominant expert, collapsing entropy to $0.184\pm0.260$, confirming Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}. Adding InstanceNorm2d (A) or BatchNorm2d (D) \emph{prevents} collapse ($1.48\pm0.02$), the \emph{opposite} of time series. Why: InstanceNorm2d strips per-channel spatial mean/variance, which carry negligible routing information for the CIFAR-10 classification task we tested (analogous to Traffic, $R{=}0.14$).
1822:\caption{\textbf{Imputation task} (20\% masked reconstruction, MOMENT-small frozen, test MSE mean$\pm$std over 5 seeds). RR-MoA wins \textbf{7/8 datasets} ($-21\%$ to $-79\%$); the sole loss is Solar where dense\_mlp dominates.}
1835:Exchange    & $1.176 \pm 0.095$ & $\mathbf{0.266 \pm 0.259}$ & $-77\%$ \\
1836:Solar       & $\mathbf{0.025 \pm 0.006}$ & $0.038 \pm 0.009$ & $+55\%$ \\

---bib timer-xl entry above; checking if there are any license-related notes:
21:\usepackage{enumitem}
234:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Normalization in Time Series.} RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} mitigates distribution shift; DishTS~\citep{fan2023dishts} and Non-stationary Transformers~\citep{liu2022nonstationary} partially recover stripped statistics. Concurrently, \citet{berthelier2026revin} independently show that several RevIN components are redundant or detrimental, and \citet{zou2025ibnorm} argue that variance-centric normalization (BatchNorm, LayerNorm, RMSNorm) is suboptimal for representation learning because two representations with identical mean and variance can encode different amounts of task-relevant information. We identify a specific, mechanistic consequence of this principle that has not been characterized in the MoE literature: when the stripped statistics carry the \emph{routing} signal in a downstream MoE adapter, instance normalization causes the router to collapse to a single expert under unfreezing, a failure mode distinct from the optimization-induced collapse studied in standard MoE rescue literature. Section~\ref{sec:method} formalizes this diagnosis and presents the minimal architectural fix it implies.}
592:    enlarge x limits=0.12,
658:\section{Conclusion and Limitations}
661:MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization needed for distribution shift also strips the per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it, motivates Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}, and $R(\mathcal{D})$ identifies where it applies. The broader pattern: when a foundation model's input pipeline destroys signal a downstream router needs, the fix is restoring the input, not retuning the optimizer (replicated in a vision control, Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}). \textbf{Limits:} \emph{(i)}~instance-level normalization only (attention-based/learned normalizers uncharacterized); \emph{(ii)}~$R(\mathcal{D})$ is a single monotone proxy, not sharp on shape-statistics-coupled data (Solar, $R{=}0.06$); \emph{(iii)}~forecasting and imputation only.\label{page:end_main}
876:Andrew~G. Howard, Menglong Zhu, Bo Chen, Dmitry Kalenichenko, Weijun Wang, Tobias Weyand, Marco Andreetto, and Hartwig Adam.
1101:\emph{Distribution-free cross-check.} Because canonical correlations capture only linear dependence, we cross-validate with the KSG $k$-nearest-neighbor MI estimator~\citep{kraskov2004ksg}, which requires no distributional assumptions. We project $S$ onto its 2 canonical variates with respect to $(M,\Sigma)$ and estimate the $2$-vs-$2$ MI with $k{=}5$ neighbors on $N{\geq}888$ samples per dataset. The KSG estimates agree with the Gaussian reference within $\pm 0.26$ nats across all 9 datasets. \emph{Caveat.} By the data-processing inequality, $I(\text{proj}(S);(M,\Sigma)) \leq I(S;(M,\Sigma))$: the canonical-variate projection preserves all linear dependence but discards nonlinear dependence between $(M,\Sigma)$ and the directions in $S$ orthogonal to the canonical-variate subspace. The KSG cross-check therefore upper-bounds the \emph{linear} component of $\varepsilon$, not the full quantity. We mitigate this by also reporting the bound-bypassing direct measurement of $H(E\mid S)$ in Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}, which makes no projection or distributional assumption and confirms the conclusions are robust.
1120:    \item[\textup{(a)}] \textbf{Heuristic SNR onset.} Setting $V_\mathrm{sig}(\alpha) = V_\mathrm{noise}(\alpha)$ in the limit $V_0 \to 0$ gives
1130:Under $M\perp\!\!\!\perp\Sigma\perp\!\!\!\perp S$ jointly Gaussian, $\mathbf{x}_\alpha = (1{-}\alpha)(M + \Sigma S) + \alpha S$. The location-scale component $(1{-}\alpha)(M+\Sigma S)$ contributes routing-discriminative variance $V_\mathrm{sig}(\alpha) = (1{-}\alpha)\,[\mathrm{Var}(M){+}\mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)]$ (the $(1{-}\alpha)$ rather than $(1{-}\alpha)^2$ scaling reflects each component's marginal contribution to a linear-readout router's input variance, see Remark below); the shape component contributes routing-irrelevant variance $V_\mathrm{noise}(\alpha) = \alpha\,\overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)} + V_0$. \textbf{Part (a):} Setting $V_\mathrm{sig}(\alpha^*) = V_\mathrm{noise}(\alpha^*)$ in the limit $V_0\to 0$ gives $(1{-}\alpha^*)[\mathrm{Var}(M){+}\mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)] = \alpha^* \overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$. Dividing both sides by $\overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ and substituting $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M){+}\mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)]/\overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ yields $(1{-}\alpha^*)R = \alpha^*$, so $\alpha^* = R/(R+1)$. Continuity of MSE in $\alpha$ for $\alpha\in[0,1)$ follows from continuity of the softmax composed with a linear function of $\alpha$. \textbf{Part (b):} At $\alpha=1$, $\mathbf{x}_1 = S$ and the routing logits are $\mathbf{w}^\top G_\psi(S)$, a deterministic function of $S$ alone; under $(M,\Sigma)\perp\!\!\!\perp S$, by the data processing inequality $I(S; E_\text{optimal}) \leq I(S; (M,\Sigma)) = 0$, so the router carries zero information about the latent location-scale that drives the optimal routing decision. The softmax of a $S$-only function converges to a single distribution at the population level, decoupling entropy from MSE. The discontinuity at $\alpha=1$ is the limit $V_\mathrm{sig}(1) = 0$ rather than a finite-$\alpha^*$ phase transition.
1867:All 30 runs converge to $\alpha < 0.5$ (range $0.445$--$0.491$), with trajectories monotonically decreasing from the $0.492$ initialization. The unanimity is the key finding: gradient descent independently discovers that raw input carries more routing information, confirming the qualitative prediction of Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}. The narrow converged range ($0.459$--$0.482$) means the magnitude does not strongly differentiate datasets: the signal is binary (raw $>$ norm), not graded.

| **"ETT/Weather/Electricity/Traffic/Solar/Exchange ... from Zhou [40], Godahewa [41]"** | **⚠ Imprecise primary attribution.** ETT correctly to Zhou (Informer); Weather typically to Wu et al. ## Summary

**Item 11 (Safeguards)** — verified, no edit. Largest adapter is 466K (SR-MoA dense), all under the 500K bound; no generative outputs.

**Item 12 (Licenses)** — tightened on two fronts:
1. **Per-model licenses now point to the exact HuggingFace identifier used in the code** (`AutonLab/MOMENT-1-small/large`, `Salesforce/moirai-1.1-R-small`, `Salesforce/moirai-moe-1.0-R-small`, `amazon/chronos-t5-small`, `thuml/timer-base-84m`). This way a reviewer can verify each license against the actual artifact, not a vague claim. Timer-XL specifically points to "the license stated on its model card" — this is the honest move when I cannot verify the exact license string from offline sources.
2. **Dataset attribution now distinguishes ETT (Zhou primary) from the remaining benchmarks (aggregated in Monash, with parenthetical credit to Wu/Autoformer for Weather and Lai/LSTNet for Traffic/Solar/Exchange as the original sources)**. The Monash citation stays as the standardized-redistribution anchor since it's what reproducibility-conscious reviewers will check.

The strictly-true new claim is also more reviewer-defensible: each license claim is paired with the exact artifact identifier, and the dataset paragraph admits the multi-source provenance instead of compressing six benchmarks into two citations.

Card explicitly states "for research purposes only in support of an academic paper." |
| **Moirai-MoE-1.0-R-small** | Apache 2.0 | **CC-BY-NC-4.0** (HF model card); uni2ts code repo is Apache 2.0 | https://huggingface.co/Salesforce/moirai-moe-1.0-R-small | **MISMATCH — same as above (non-commercial)**. |
| **Chronos-T5-small** | Apache 2.0 | **Apache 2.0** | https://huggingface.co/amazon/chronos-t5-small + https://github.com/amazon-science/chronos-forecasting/blob/main/LICENSE | OK |
| **Timer-XL / timer-base-84m** | "publicly released terms" (vague) | **Apache 2.0** | https://huggingface.co/thuml/timer-base-84m + https://github.com/thuml/Large-Time-Series-Model | OK — replace vague phrasing with "Apache 2.0" |

## Datasets

| Item | Claimed | Actually | Source URL | Discrepancy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **ETT (h1/h2/m1/m2)** | Zhou et al. 2021 (Informer, AAAI) | Citation correct. License: **CC-BY-ND 4.0** (NoDerivatives) | https://github.com/zhouhaoyi/ETDataset/blob/main/LICENSE | **License flag — CC-BY-ND prohibits distribution of modified/derivative datasets**. If the paper redistributes preprocessed ETT splits (e.g., normalized arrays in `data/`), this may technically violate ND. Citation is correct (Informer @ AAAI 2021). |
| **Weather** (LTSF benchmark) | Wu et al. 2021 (Autoformer) for the LTSF preprocessing; raw data from **MPI Biogeochemistry Jena** | Original recordings: MPI-BGC Jena weather station (https://www.bgc-jena.mpg.de/wetter/). **No explicit license** on the MPI-BGC page. The LTSF "Weather" preprocessed version is redistributed via the Autoformer repo. Monash archive includes a "Weather" dataset attributed to Sparks et al. 2020, which is a different dataset. | https://www.bgc-jena.mpg.de/wetter/ | **License unverified upstream**. Cite both MPI-BGC Jena (data source) and Wu et al. 2021 Autoformer (LTSF preprocessing/benchmark form). Do NOT cite Monash for Weather — the Monash "Weather" is Sparks et al. 2020, not the Jena weather station. |
| **Electricity (ECL, 321 ch)** | UCI ElectricityLoadDiagrams20112014 | Confirmed. **CC-BY-4.0**. Creator: Artur Trindade (2015). DOI: 10.24432/C58C86 | https://archive.ics.uci.edu/dataset/321/electricityloaddiagrams20112014 | OK — but the paper should cite **Trindade 2015** as the data creator, not just attribute via LSTNet/Informer. |
| **Traffic (PeMS, 862 ch)** | Caltrans PeMS / Lai 2018 LSTNet | Source confirmed (California PeMS via Caltrans). **No public license on PeMS** — requires free account registration; conditions of use page exists but terms are not openly published. | https://pems.dot.ca.gov/ + https://github.com/laiguokun/multivariate-time-series-data | **License unverified — flag for honesty**. Say "Caltrans PeMS, public after free registration; redistributed via Lai et al. 2018." |
| **Solar (137 stations)** | NREL / Lai 2018 LSTNet | Origin: NREL Solar Power Data for Integration Studies (2006, 137 PV plants in Alabama). NREL data is generally US Government work (effectively public domain in the US), but specific dataset terms not surfaced via NREL search. | https://github.com/laiguokun/multivariate-time-series-data | License unverified. Cite Lai et al. 2018 + NREL as original source. |
| **Exchange (8 currencies)** | Lai 2018 LSTNet | Confirmed as introduced/redistributed via LSTNet repo. **No LICENSE file in `laiguokun/multivariate-time-series-data` repo.** | https://github.com/laiguokun/multivariate-time-series-data | **License unverified — no LICENSE file in source repo**. Underlying exchange-rate data is public-record, but the curated 8-currency CSV has no stated license. |
| **Monash Archive** | Godahewa et al. 2021 (NeurIPS 2021 datasets track) | Citation correct. Per-dataset Zenodo records use **CC-BY-4.0**. The aggregated forecastingdata.org site states "intended for research purpose only" but per-record Zenodo licenses are CC-BY-4.0. Archive contains Electricity, Traffic-SF (Caltrans/PeMS, 862), Solar (137), and "Weather" (Sparks et al. 2020 — NOT MPI-BGC Jena). | https://forecastingdata.org/ + https://zenodo.org/communities/forecasting | **Flag — Monash "Weather" ≠ LTSF "Weather"** (different sources). Do not claim Monash redistributes the MPI-BGC Jena weather data. Exchange Rate is also in Monash. |

## Summary of action items for the paper

1. **CRITICAL — Moirai and Moirai-MoE are CC-BY-NC-4.0**, not Apache 2.0. The HF model cards explicitly restrict to research/non-commercial use. Update §License/Ethics section accordingly. The `uni2ts` codebase is Apache 2.0, but the **weights** are CC-BY-NC-4.0 — these are separate licenses.
2. **Timer-XL is Apache 2.0** — replace "publicly released terms" with the concrete license.
3. **ETT is CC-BY-ND 4.0** — if `data/` ships normalized/derived ETT arrays, this is a potential ND violation. Safest fix: distribute only loader scripts, not derivative arrays.
4. **Disambiguate "Weather"**: the LTSF Weather (MPI-BGC Jena via Autoformer) is NOT the Monash "Weather" (Sparks et al. 2020). Cite MPI-BGC + Autoformer for the LTSF version.
5. **Electricity citation should include Trindade 2015** (UCI dataset creator), not just LSTNet/Informer.
6. **PeMS Traffic, Solar, Exchange have no clearly-stated public license** — disclose this honestly rather than asserting permissive terms.

## Critical findings from the web verification + corrections applied

| # | Was wrong | Reality | Action |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Moirai/Moirai-MoE: **Apache 2.0** | **CC-BY-NC-4.0** for the model weights (uni2ts code is Apache 2.0 — separate license) | Updated to disclose CC-BY-NC-4.0 weight license; explicit statement that our use is academic research consistent with the non-commercial restriction |
| 2 | Timer-XL: "publicly released terms" (vague) | **Apache 2.0** (`thuml/timer-base-84m` HF model card) | Concrete license now stated |
| 3 | ETT: no license info | **CC-BY-ND-4.0** (NoDerivatives) on `zhouhaoyi/ETDataset` | Disclosed; noted that we ship loader scripts only (`data/` is in `.gitignore`, confirmed not redistributed) |
| 4 | Weather attributed to Zhou+Godahewa | Actually **MPI-BGC Jena** raw data, redistributed for LTSF by **Wu et al. (Autoformer)**. The Monash "Weather" is a different dataset (Sparks et al. 2020) | Re-attributed correctly; added `wu2021autoformer` bibitem |
| 5 | Electricity attributed via the same hand-wave | UCI `ElectricityLoadDiagrams20112014` (CC-BY-4.0, **Trindade 2015** as creator) | Now cited specifically with license |
| 6 | Traffic/Solar/Exchange: implied permissive | Caltrans PeMS / NREL / curated by **Lai et al. 2018 (LSTNet)** with **no explicit redistribution license** on the curated CSVs | Honest disclosure that source data is public-record but curated CSVs lack a stated license; we ship loader scripts only; added `lai2018lstnet` bibitem |
| 7 | Monash claim was overbroad | Monash IS CC-BY-4.0 and IS the right reproducibility anchor for several datasets, but does NOT contain the LTSF Weather | Re-positioned as "recommended reproducibility anchor" rather than primary source |

**The Moirai license correction is the most important** — claiming Apache 2.0 when the weights are CC-BY-NC-4.0 is a real licensing inaccuracy. Now the checklist explicitly says "Our use is academic research, consistent with the non-commercial weight license", which is honest and reviewer-defensible.

The two missing bibitems (`wu2021autoformer`, `lai2018lstnet`) have been added to the bibliography. Build still passes, 47 pages, no undefined references.

A single RR-MoA run takes $\sim$1--3 minutes per dataset on MOMENT-small, $\sim$1.5 minutes on MOMENT-large, and similar on Moirai/Moirai-MoE/Chronos/Timer-XL. The full experimental suite ($\sim$5{,}000 result files across the main tables, baselines, rescue sweep, cross-backbone, multi-horizon, imputation, and ablations) accumulated approximately 100--200 GPU-hours of compute end-to-end. Inference latency benchmarks (Table~\ref{tab:benchmark}) report wall-clock measurements for each architectural variant.
+    \item[] Justification: All experiments were conducted on a single NVIDIA A10G GPU (23\,GB VRAM, CUDA 12.4). Per-run training wall-clock (15 epochs, batch 128) is recorded by every experiment script into the per-run result JSON via an \texttt{elapsed} field; the figures below are \emph{measured} medians from the released metadata, not estimates. By backbone: MOMENT-small $1$--$2$ minutes per dataset (median across $n{=}1{,}094$ runs); MOMENT-large $\sim$3--3.5 minutes ($n{=}15$); Chronos $\sim$15--30 seconds ($n{=}42$); Moirai $\sim$25--30 seconds ($n{=}78$); Timer-XL $\sim$35--40 seconds ($n{=}90$); Moirai-MoE $\sim$1.5--4 minutes ($n{=}66$, sparse-MoE backbone, slowest of the set). The full experimental suite is 5{,}463 result JSON files; summing the \texttt{elapsed} field across the 5{,}045 files that carry it yields a measured total of \textbf{234.8 GPU-hours} of training compute end-to-end (this excludes orchestrator overhead, model loading, and the few hundred files from legacy runs that pre-date the \texttt{elapsed} convention). Inference latency benchmarks (Table~\ref{tab:benchmark}) report wall-clock measurements for each architectural variant. Both numbers can be re-derived from the released artifacts: the per-run JSONs in \texttt{results/} carry the \texttt{elapsed} field, and the inference benchmark is reproduced by \texttt{scripts/benchmark\_inference.py}.

 \item {\bf Licenses for existing assets}
     \item[] Question: Are the creators or original owners of assets (e.g., code, data, models), used in the paper, properly credited and are the license and terms of use explicitly mentioned and properly respected?
     \item[] Answer: \answerYes{}
-    \item[] Justification: All pretrained models (MOMENT, Moirai, Moirai-MoE, Chronos, Timer-XL) and datasets (ETT, Weather, Electricity, Traffic, Solar, Exchange) are cited with their original papers. MOMENT is released under MIT license; Moirai/Moirai-MoE under Apache 2.0; Chronos under Apache 2.0; Timer-XL under its publicly released terms. ETT/Weather/Electricity/Traffic/Solar/Exchange datasets are standard public LTSF benchmarks from~\citet{zhou2021informer, godahewa2021monash}.
+    \item[] Justification: All pretrained models and datasets are cited with their original papers in the bibliography. \textbf{Pretrained models (with license).} MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}: MIT, \texttt{AutonLab/MOMENT-1-small} and \texttt{AutonLab/MOMENT-1-large} on Hugging Face. Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} and Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe}: model \emph{weights} are released under CC-BY-NC-4.0 (research/non-commercial use only) on \texttt{Salesforce/moirai-1.1-R-small} and \texttt{Salesforce/moirai-moe-1.0-R-small}; the supporting \texttt{uni2ts} code is Apache 2.0. Our use is academic research, consistent with the non-commercial weight license. Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}: Apache 2.0, \texttt{amazon/chronos-t5-small}. Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}: Apache 2.0, \texttt{thuml/timer-base-84m}. \textbf{Datasets.} ETT (ETTh1/h2/m1/m2) is introduced by~\citet{zhou2021informer} and released under CC-BY-ND-4.0 (NoDerivatives); we ship only loader scripts and do not redistribute preprocessed arrays. Weather is the MPI-BGC Jena meteorological recording, redistributed for the LTSF benchmark by Wu et al.~\citep{wu2021autoformer}. Electricity is the UCI \texttt{ElectricityLoadDiagrams20112014} dataset (CC-BY-4.0, originally collected by Artur Trindade). Traffic (Caltrans PeMS), Solar (NREL Solar Power Data), and Exchange (multi-currency exchange rates) were curated for time series benchmarking by Lai et al.~\citep{lai2018lstnet}; the source data is public-record, but the curated benchmark CSVs do not ship with an explicit redistribution license, so we again only ship loader scripts pointing to the upstream sources. The Monash time series forecasting archive~\citep{godahewa2021monash} (CC-BY-4.0) provides standardized versions of several of these datasets and is the recommended reproducibility anchor. No dataset is used outside the standardized LTSF forecasting and imputation tasks for which it was collected.
